Now the wait for the buffer is bounded: once the oldest queued turn has waited `target + 1` turns' time the runner goes on with what it has. **Measured**: the real client against a fake room whose last turn came 60 / 100 / 150 / 300 ms late acknowledged turn 198 of 199 and never ran the last; now 199 in all of them (and at 0 ms); the runner on 19 shapes of late turns near the end of a stream: the last one or two turns unexecuted in 9 of 19 before, none after; 400 matches of two guests over a spiky link (a message held up by 120 - 250 ms with probability 0.2 - 5 %) in each of eight rows: 6 - 59 of 800 guests (0.75 - 7.4 %) did not reach the referee's final tick before, **none of 6,400 now, in a server's room and in a game with a host's seat alike**. Tests: `test_jitter` J2.9 (the 19 shapes, three frame phases each), J2.10 (the bound: 150 ms for a buffer of two, counted from the oldest turn, never for a runner that has not started), `test_lockstep` S2.10 (sixty matches, 120 guests: 9 of them miss the final tick without the fix).
  - **The ping readout was quantized to the frame period.** It was measured with the frame's clock at both ends, so it included the frame in which the answer was read: 11 - 30 ms too high at 30 frames a second, 31 - 64 ms at 15, about a second in a page that is hardly drawn. The browser build stamps every WebSocket message with `emscripten_get_now()` in the event that delivers it, and `PingMeter::on_pong` takes off the time that the Pong waited to be read (`Connection::last_message_age_ms`, the loopback network of the tests reports the same); a Pong that waited longer than the frame clock has run since its ping (a hidden tab: the application hands the network at most a second of a frame) is no measurement. A reading whose last answer is older than three seconds is shown as `ping -` (the host answers nothing, the link is stuck). **A native window still reads up to one frame (16.7 ms at 60 frames a second, 8 on average) too high**: its socket is read when the frame polls it; polling it while the frame limiter sleeps was weighed and not done (`docs/NETWORK_PORT.md`). **Measured** (the closed loop of two real sessions over a link of 10 and of 30 ms each way, the readout against the link's round trip, 8.3 / 16.7 / 33.3 / 66.7 / 100 / 250 / 500 / 1000 ms frames): before +5 / +13 / +13 / +47 / +80 / +230 / +480 / +980 ms, after +0 - 1 at every frame period; in a real browser (headless Chrome, the game's frame loop at 60 / 30 / 15 / 1 frames a second, a relay adding 0 and 30 ms each way, the wire log of the page as the truth, two runs each): the ping's error against the wire at 60 / 30 / 15 / 1 frames a second with nothing added: +11.6 / +30.4 / +63.8 / +996.4 ms before, +0.0 / +0.0 / +0.1 / +0.2 ms after (with 30 ms added each way: +6.0 / +12.6 / +32.1 / +926.3 before, +0.1 / 0.0 / +0.1 / 0.0 after); all 224 readings of the fixed build lie within -2.9 and +0.9 ms of the wire (99.6 % within 1 ms), none of the 224 of the first version did (their mean error was +260 ms); the delay readout is the real time from the command to the tick to within -0.2 ms on the mean (a second round on the images of the finished release, 98 readings: ping error -0.8 .. +0.9 ms, delay error -1.5 .. +0.7 ms); the readout and the wire at every frame rate are in the table of `docs/NETWORK_PORT.md`. Tests: `test_latency` N9.31 (the arithmetic and the staleness), N9.32 (every frame rate: exactly twice the one-way delay), N9.33 (a page that was not drawn for 20 s), N9.34 (the age of a message), N9.35 (the room's ping), `test_network_app` N5.31 (the application: "ping -" after 3.7 s without an answer, a number again when the host is back).
  - **A window drawn less often than every 100 ms never ran a backlog down.** A long frame added its time at normal speed, so the turns that had piled up before it (a window that was frozen, a hidden tab that came back) stayed for ever: a page drawn once a second stood 1.95 s behind the match for good and one drawn every 1.5 - 2 s, whose clock the application cuts at one second a frame, fell further behind every second until the server dropped it. A long frame now runs the turns that its own time covers and the backlog beyond them at up to 4x (the same rule as a short frame), and may run as many ticks as its time holds at that speed (8 for each 100 ms: 80 for the second that the application hands the network at most). **Measured** (a room with one window drawn every 1 / 1.5 / 2 s, the server's view by the acknowledgements): 1.95 s behind for ever / 4.95 s after 10 s and 41.95 s after 120 s / 6.95 s after 10 s, dropped at 116 s before; 1.0 / 1.5 / 2.0 s, level for good, after. In headless Chrome a page drawn once a second from its first frame stood 1.05 s behind with a delay readout of 2,000 ms and now stands 50 ms behind and reads 1,000 ms, and a page that was hidden for 15 s and then drawn once a second came back 15.1 s behind and stayed so for the next minute, and is level 4 s after its first frame now. A window drawn less often than once in 4 s still cannot keep up (a second of the match at 4x is 4 s). Tests: `test_jitter` J2.11 (150 / 250 / 500 / 1000 ms frames), J2.8 and `test_lockstep` S2.7 changed with it (the first frame back from a freeze runs 80 ticks, and "Catching up..." shows for a freeze of 9 s: for 5 s the first frame runs most of it).
  - **One freeze of the link is not jitter** (tuning). The turns that a freeze held back come in one bunch whose first turn waited the whole freeze: counted in the 95th percentile, the bunch made the buffer four turns for ten seconds and then a turn fewer every ten seconds, half a minute of 100 - 150 ms more delay for a link that froze once, for a buffer of 200 ms that could not have bridged it anyway. A wait of 400 ms or more (as the runner counts it) is a freeze, and the turns read in the next 500 ms are left out of the percentile; waits of 1.5 s or more forget what was read before, as before; shorter waits, recurring spikes and jittery links are counted as ever, and the buffer takes no step down on a link whose history was just forgotten. **Measured** with the closed-loop driver (a scripted link, 60 Hz frames, 300 s): one freeze of 1.5 / 3 / 5 / 10 s moved the buffer 1 -> 4 -> 1 (four changes, the mean wait from arrival to tick 70 / 72 / 83 / 124 ms, 95th percentile 163 - 213 ms) and now leaves it at one turn (58 / 64 / 74 / 116 ms, 95th percentile 66 ms); a freeze of 1.2 s every 5 s: mean wait 208 -> 102 ms, one of 3 s every 5 s: 364 -> 329 ms, the same 57 stalls: the other rows (a steady link, jitter of 20 - 300 ms, periodic freezes of 120 and 400 ms, a link that alternates, steps, ramps and clock rates) are byte for byte what they were. Tests: `test_jitter` J1.10, J2.16 (a jittery link that freezes keeps the buffer that its jitter needs).
  - **The delay readout recovers after a stop** (it stayed at 1 - 1.1 s for about 25 s): it is the median of the last five commands now (it was the mean of ten): one command that waited out a stall does not show, three fresh ones bring it back (7.5 s at an order every 2.5 s; a second or two for a player who is busy), and it says `delay -` when the last command was applied more than ten seconds ago (the ping: three seconds): a page that was hidden no longer shows the numbers of before. The frame rate counter shows the real rate down to one frame a second (it read "10 FPS" for every rate under 10: the frame was clamped at 100 ms for the local simulation). `docker/nginx.conf` sent two `Cache-Control` headers for favicon.png, the sprites, the sounds and the images (the `expires` directive adds one of its own): one now, checked with `curl -I` on the built image (and the revalidation headers of the game's files are as they were). Tests: `test_latency` N9.10, N9.29, N9.30, N9.36, N9.13; `test_network_app` N5.27 (the dash and its return); `test_app_integration` 9.5.
  - **A laggard is told that it lags.** The `Lag` notice went to everybody but the laggard, and "Catching up..." needs the backlog in the player's own queue: a player whose own link is slow has its backlog on the way, saw nothing and was not told until the drop at 60 s. The notice goes to the whole room now, the laggard included, and its screen says "You are lagging (12 s behind)" (below "Catching up...", above "Bob is lagging"); it is kept apart from the notices about the others and is stale after 10 s. A throttled downlink in the test (a third of the stream's speed): the first notice reached the player 13.5 s after its link slowed down, 40 s into it the player was 27.9 s behind and read 8.4 s: the notice is late and its number old, but the player knows; in headless Chrome with a link of 4 s added each way for 45 s the page said "You are lagging (4 s behind)" at 8 s and "(8 s behind)" from 12 s on, with `ping -` in the corner from 3 s to 8 s. Tests: `test_lockstep` S2.11 (and S2.8, which pinned that the laggard is NOT told), `test_hud_layout` (the line and its priority).
  - **A finished room waits for its laggards.** The room stopped updating its session when the match ended, so it answered no ping and closed the links 15 s later, and a client that has not run the last turn gives up on a server that is silent for 10 s: a player 40 - 60 s behind at the end (15 s of catch-up at 4x) was cut off in the middle of it and never saw the results. The room now goes on answering and taking acknowledgements until every player that is still connected has acknowledged the last turn, for at least the 15 s of before and at most 30 s (`Room::kEndWaitMs`). Test: `test_server` S3.34 (a player 55 s behind: answered all the time, runs every turn to the last; with the old room it was cut off 10 s after the end), S3.35 (the two bounds).
  - **A burst of orders is carried, not refused** (an old defect of the sequencer, found by the review). A turn takes 64 commands of one player; a stuck uplink that is let go (5 s stuck at 15 orders a second: 76 orders in one burst) hit the cap, the 12 refused orders were 12 violations and 8 throw a player out. The commands beyond a turn's 64 now wait in a queue of the player's own (up to 256, four turns' worth) and go into the next turns in the order they were sent; only a peer with more waiting than that is refused. The sequencer alone decides which turn carries a command, so every machine runs the same turns. Tests: `test_lockstep` N2.5b, S2.13 (76 orders: the player stays, no violation, all run in order, the turn logs of the server and of every client are byte for byte the same), N2.5 and `test_ai` AI5.3 (the flood is refused beyond 64 + 256 now).
  - **A dropped player is told why.** A player dropped for not acknowledging for 30 s (a hidden tab, a process that was stopped) found "The connection to the other players was lost." A machine that finds its link closed with 20 s or more of the match unplayed in its queue now says "You were away too long and were dropped from the match." (no protocol change: it knows from what it holds; a link that fails by itself, or a server that goes silent, still gets the old line). Test: `test_lockstep` S2.12. **A real server with real clients** (`SIGSTOP`): one of two or four native clients of a room of the real `ants_server` stopped for 5, 20 and 35 s: the room ran 19.9 - 20.1 ticks a second before, during and after every stop; the others read "C1 is lagging (3 s behind)" 2.9 s after the stop and one more second every second; after 5 s the stopped player read "You are lagging (5 s behind)" for 0.07 s as it ran again and the others' notice ended with it; after 20 s it showed "Catching up..." for 5.0 s and the others' notice counted down 20, 13, 10, 7, 4 s and ended 5.0 s after it ran again; after 35 s it had been dropped at 30.0 s (the notice of all three others ended then) and said "You were away too long and were dropped from the match." the moment it ran again.
  - **Smaller things.** `HostSession` and `ClientSession` are neither copyable nor movable (their runner hooks capture `this`; a `static_assert` in `test_lockstep` pins it). The `FloodPeer` of `test_server` built a frame with a range insert that GCC 12 at `-O3 -Werror` reads as an overread (found by the review: the project's own build failed there); a byte loop, and the new `MedianWindow` uses an insertion sort for the same compiler's sake (`std::sort` on a window of five made GCC 12 report `-Warray-bounds`). `web/four.html` said that a hidden or covered window "stops playing and the match waits for it": nothing has waited since this release (the others play on, it is called lagging after 3 s and dropped after 30 s without a sign of life, and it cannot come back until the reconnect release): the hint says so (checked by `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh`). Pinned by tests that the review found missing (each fails when the thing it pins is broken): a stall that begins in a long frame is counted from the frames that follow, so that "Waiting for the other players..." appears (L1, `test_jitter` J2.12); a stall that ends in a long frame is not told to the rule (J2.13); owed time is bounded (J2.14); `backlog_ms()` counts a turn as 50 ms, the unit of the "Catching up..." threshold of 60 turns (J2.15, S2.14); the acknowledgement of a frame that ran many turns is for the last of them; a stale notice goes after 3 s; a late pass seals at most ten turns; the server's host never waits and names no laggard (S2.14).
  - **The headline delays, honestly.** The delays of this entry (91 / 134 / 164 ms; 188 / 235 / 292 before) were measured with headless native clients, and a headless client skips the frame limiter (`Application::run`): it polls the socket thousands of times a second, so its readout has none of the frame rounding that a player's window has (the earlier text called them "paced at 60 frames a second"; they were not). **A window at 60 frames a second** (the web build in headless Chrome, the page in front, 60 Hz) reads **99.5 ms at a round trip of 3 ms and 162.5 ms at one of 71 ms** (the median of the last five commands against the page's wire log, two runs each, a relay adding 0 and 30 ms each way; 98.9 and 167.9 ms at 30 frames a second, 133.8 and 192.4 at 15, 1,000 at one frame a second): 17 - 21 ms more than the round trip + 25 + 50 that an unpaced client reads, which are its frames (a click waits for the frame that sends it, a turn for the frame that runs it); the frames are the same before and after this release, so the saving of about 100 ms is the same. Both are stated in `README.md` and `docs/NETWORK_PORT.md`, which also had the lag tests under their old numbers (S2.5 - S2.7 are S2.7 - S2.9), the leader's section under protocol 7 (`StartRequest` is not `MsgType::Last` any more), a table row that said "turn at the relay to the tick" (it is the turn read at the relay, a few ms after it was sealed, to the tick), "at most 20 acknowledgements a second" (80 at 4x) and the built-in browser pane's frame rate as if it were Chrome's (Chrome draws a hidden tab not at all: "A hidden tab" in the document has what a tab does, measured in headless Chrome, a tab hidden for 5.5 / 20.5 / 40.5 s: it drew no frame and sent nothing, the room ran 20.09 / 19.99 / 20.01 ticks a second, the others read "Target is lagging (3 s behind)" from 2.9 s, the tabs that came back were level with the match 0.2 s and 5.2 s after their first frame (1.2 s and 6.2 s before the fix of the long frames) and the one that was away for 40.5 s had been dropped at 30.0 s and showed the setup screen with "You were away too long and were dropped from the match.").
  - **Not fixed here, written down** (`docs/NETWORK_PORT.md`, "Limits of the lag policy and the readout"): a client that acknowledges turns it has not run is never called lagging (it gains nothing in the simulation, the referee's hash comparison names a machine that really diverges); a reloaded page or a closed window forfeits its seat (the reconnect release); a rejected join is invisible behind the quick help on the web page (the start menu release does the desktop); a window drawn less often than once in 4 s cannot keep up; the ping of a native window reads up to a frame high.
  - **Mutants** (a scratch script, not in the repository, that applies one change at a time to a copy of the tree and runs the suites that should notice; each mutant one change of the fixed code; **52 mutants, 50 killed by a test and 2 by the compiler (the `static_assert`s), none survives**): the review's survivors L1, L12, L13, L14, L19, S2, S3, S10, S16, S18, S19 and the mutants of every fix above: the bound of the rebuild wait removed, three times as long, or applied to a runner that has not started; long frames at normal speed; the old tick cap; no freeze skip, a freeze that is a wait of 1.5 s, no skip window, a step down without evidence; the delay as the last command's or a median of ten; the age of a Pong ignored by the session, by the lobby, believed when it is beyond the clock, not reported by the loopback; a reading that is never stale (in the corner, in NetGame); the answer's time without its age; the frame counter clamped again; the laggard not told, the line missing or below the others'; the lost reason always or never "away", the threshold above what a dropped player holds, the generic text; the room not answering after the end, closing at the grace period, never closing, closing when level without the grace period; no carry, a carry that is not moved on, one that a gone player keeps, one that is not bounded; the sessions copyable.
- Tests: new suite 2.24 (`test_jitter`, 26 tests, 1,442 assertions, nine of them (J1.10, J2.9 - J2.16) added by the review fixes below: the rule's numbers, a steady link at every frame rate gives one turn, jitter of 30 / 60 / 120 / 200 ms gives 1 - 2 / 2 - 3 / 3 - 4 / 4 turns, a stall grows the buffer only when a turn would bridge it, a spike under 5 % of the turns is ignored, hitches and the first update are not counted, the shrink is a turn per 10 s and never two together, a wait of 1.5 s and a frame of 1 s forget, the runner's speed is a function of the slack and never beyond 4x, ten random jittery links are learned in seconds, a frozen window leaves no large buffer); new suite 2.23 (`test_latency`, 36 tests, 107,437 assertions, eight of them (N9.29 - N9.36) added by the review fixes: the meters, a host and a guest over a simulated link of 40 ms each way, the 50 ms model exact for all 100 phases of a turn: 130 - 179 ms, the host's own 50 - 99 ms, and what the player sees when the game waits); `test_lockstep` 45 -> 55 tests, 125,770 assertions (**S2.7** a client of a server stops for 5 s: the others' ticks go on at the normal rate, they are told, it catches up at up to 4x and every hash agrees; **S2.8** the numbers of the lag policy on raw links; **S2.9** a round trip of 200 ms and of 200 - 400 ms are never lag; **N2.39** what an honest client sends against the flood budget; **S2.10 - S2.14 and N2.5b** are the review fixes' (sixty matches over a spiky link, the laggard told, the dropped player's reason, a burst of orders carried, the small rules of the lag policy, the sequencer's carry); mutation-checked: S2.7 and S2.8 fail when the server waits again, S2.9 with a notice threshold of 300 ms, J2.8 of `test_jitter` without the clock rule); `test_server` 32 -> 35 tests, 10,277 assertions (**S3.33** the leader's window stops after the early START; **S3.34** and **S3.35** the review fixes': a laggard at the end of a match, the bounds of the room's wait); `test_tcp` 7 -> 8 tests, 72,267 assertions (**N3.8**); `test_ws` 22 -> 23 tests (**W1.23**); `test_hud_layout` 946 -> 1,038 checks (the readout 70, the overlay lines 22); `test_network_app` 33 -> 38 tests, 750 assertions (**N5.27 - N5.31**, the latency tests, N5.31 the review fixes': the dash and its return; N5.20 - N5.26 are the leader's); `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` 81 -> 83 checks. The full `./run_tests.sh` passes (every suite, 506 / 506 E2E).
- Rewritten tests (all because they pinned turns of 100 ms, two ticks, a buffer of two turns, the room that waits, or protocol 7; none removed, disabled or weakened): `test_lockstep` **N2.2** (24 is `StartRequest` and 25 is `Lag`: the unknown types start at 26 and `MsgType::Last` is 25), **N2.3** (a plausible type byte runs to 25), **N2.3b** (it asserted protocol 7: now 7 or later), **N2.8** (the runner: two ticks per turn, a buffer of two turns and a catch-up at double speed became one tick per turn, a buffer that is collected again after a stall, 4x), N2.21 (one hook call per tick, one per turn), N2.36 (the log is 600 turns, 30 s; the flow control 60 turns, 3 s) and the turn counts of N2.9, N2.14, N2.15, N2.16, N2.18, N2.20, N2.22 and S2.1 (twice as many turns in the same time; the same meaning in time); `test_ai` AI5.2 (a seat that never acknowledges stalls a sequencer after 61 turns, 3 s, and the 2 minutes are 2,400 turns), AI5.4 and AI5.5 (turn counts); `test_tcp`, `test_lobby`, `test_netgame` and `test_network_app` (turn counts; N5.27 and N5.28 the ranges of the delay, 50 - 150 ms and 50 - 120 ms; N5.30 the title and the bound, eight turns of a 400 ms frame); `test_server` **S3.26** (v0.0.93: the status counts turns, one per tick now: 500 became 1000), S3.4 (turns) and **S3.12** (it pinned that a seat that stops executing turns holds the room up and is dropped after 20 s: now the room never waits, the others are told, and the seat is dropped after 30 s without an acknowledgement; the two-player room that ends and the wall-clock limit are as they were); `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` (the raw client's Hello: a literal protocol 7 became the server's own, from `--version`). **Rewritten by the review fixes, each because it pinned what the fix changed (none removed, disabled or weakened; every one still asserts what it was about): `test_jitter` J2.1 (a hold of 600 ms: no buffer bridges it and the bunch that it held back is the freeze's own lateness, so the buffer stays at one turn, where it asserted two or more), J2.8 (the first frame back from a freeze of 10 s runs 80 ticks, the second it stands for at 4x, so 100 - 125 turns stay queued, where it asserted 150 or more: the second at normal speed), J2.5 (its title says what it pins now); `test_latency` N9.10 and N9.13 (the number is the median of the last five commands, not the mean of ten: the same data, the median of it); `test_lockstep` N2.5 (one player floods the sequencer: three of ten commands were taken with a cap of three, seven are now: three and the four that wait for the next turns; the rest is refused), N2.8 (the bunch of 38 turns after a wait of 1.7 s is the freeze's own lateness: the buffer stays at one turn, where it asserted four), S2.7 (the stop is 9 s, so that "Catching up..." still shows when the first frame back runs most of a shorter backlog), S2.8 (it pinned that the laggard is NOT told: it is told now; the numbers of its table are unchanged); `test_ai` AI5.3 (63 of 100 commands were accepted in a turn; 63 + 256 of 400 are, the flood is refused beyond that); and the helpers of `test_server` (a client's link, the server end of a connection) and the byte loop of its `FloodPeer`. Extended, not rewritten: `test_hud_layout` (the line "You are lagging" and its place after "Catching up...") and `test_app_integration` 9.5 (the frame rate counter reads the real rate under ten frames a second). Where a test of this release had the number of one of v0.0.93's, the newer one was renumbered, its meaning unchanged: the lag tests of `test_lockstep` are S2.7 - S2.9 (S2.5 - S2.7 as they were written; S2.5 and S2.6 are the leader's) and the latency tests of `test_network_app` are N5.27 - N5.30 (N5.20 - N5.23 as written; N5.20 - N5.26 are the leader's). No test pins the hash of a network match (they compare the machines of a match with each other), so no hash changed.
- Documents: README (the feature table, "how a match runs", the server's lag policy, the leader, the suite rows measured from a full run; two older counts corrected: the power-up actions 4,926 and the map sweep's 62 checks), `docs/NETWORK_PORT.md` (turns, the jitter buffer, protocol 8 and its two new types, who waits for whom, the server's clock and load, the frame loop, flood control measured with 50 ms turns, recommendations), `docs/BOTS.md` (the arena's turn model), `STATUS.md`, `web/four.html` (the hint about a hidden window), the comments of `protocol.hpp` (the version history), `session.hpp`, `lockstep.hpp`, `jitter.hpp`, `flood.hpp`, `room.hpp`, `control.hpp`, `tcp.cpp`, `ws.cpp`, `latency.hpp` and `latency_corner.hpp`.

## v0.0.93 - 2026-10-01 - The room leader can start early

The owner asked: "the first player to join the lobby should get a start button in case they want to start the game with less players". A room on the game server used to wait for every seat that it was made for (two to four players, up to ten minutes for a demo room). Now **its leader, the first player who joined, can start the match with the players who are there**. Nothing else about the room or the match changed. This is **network protocol 7**, which does not speak with protocol 6 (a game of the old version is refused with "version mismatch", by a LAN host as by a server). **Three things ship together:** the leader itself, **the two fixes of its review** (one connection could flood the server with valid messages, and the quick help could press the leader's START by accident) and **the revalidation of the web files** (a reload downloaded about 9 MB again).

- **Who leads** (`HostLobby`): in a room of the dedicated server (the server holds no seat) the player who was welcomed first leads; when it leaves (it quits, is kicked, is thrown out for garbage, or its connection dies) the earliest of the players who are left leads. The order is the order of the welcomes, not the number of the seat (asking for a seat with `--seat` or `?seat=` gives no lead), a player who comes later never takes the lead, one who comes back is the newest, and when everybody has gone the next one to come leads. A computer player never does. The Room message names the leader to everybody, and the new leader is already named in the message that tells about the departure of the old one. A LAN / direct room has no leader: its host starts, as before, and its guests keep the guest's screen.
- **The wire**: `RoomMsg` has a last byte `leader` (a seat, or 255 for nobody) and there is a new message `StartRequest` (type 24, player to server, **no payload: one byte**). The decoder is strict like the others: a leader is 255 or the seat of a player who joined as a guest (an empty seat, a bot and a host are refused), a Room message of protocol 6 (no such byte) is no Room message, and a StartRequest with a payload is garbage (a violation: eight of them throw the sender out). A request that cannot be honoured is no offence: it is ignored and counted (`ignored_start_requests`), whether it comes from a player who is not the leader, from any guest of a LAN host, from a room without early start, with fewer than two players, or when the match is loading already (the leader's second click on START arrives after the Start). In a running match a server ignores a late request, a LAN host counts it as a violation.
- **The server**: `RoomSpec::early_start` (the control interface's `"early_start": true|false` in `POST /rooms`, true when left out, a value of another type is a 400; demo rooms have it on). When the leader's request arrives in a waiting room that can start (two players at least), the room starts at once with the seats that are taken (the roster is those seats; `expected` keeps what the room was made for, `joined` shows who came). A room whose seats are all taken still starts by itself. With `early_start` off the room has no leader and waits for every seat. The status of a room (`GET /rooms`, `GET /rooms/<code>`) has three new keys: `early_start`, `leader` (the seat while the room waits or loads, otherwise null) and `ignored_start_requests`. Where the early start meets the rest of the room: a request in the two seconds of pause after a cancelled start is lost (the leader presses START again), a leader who asked and left in the same breath asked for nothing, and seats that the map cannot be played by (a start marker outside the grid) are not started early and do not fail the room (a full room still fails with the reason, as it always did).
- **The leader's screen**: the first player's setup screen is the original's **host screen** (`st_screen`: Up / Down, START, Fog of War) in a **server room mode**: the map label shows the room's map (the description from this machine's copy of the file, "???" without it), Up, Down and the wrap change nothing, the Fog buttons show the room's choice and change nothing (they still show their hover and pressed pictures and click), **START (the button, `Enter`, the keypad's Enter, `S`, `s`) sends the request**, Leave works as everywhere, and the rows are the guest's (itself first: nothing jumps when the lead moves). Its prompt is the host's own text ("Press START when all players' thumbs have appeared."); no new text appears on any original screen. **With fewer than two players START does what the host's START does on a LAN room with too few players: the can't-go cue, no message, nothing sent.** Everybody else keeps the guest's screen; when the leader leaves, the next one's screen turns into the host's with the next Room message. When the server's Start arrives every screen is locked (a second click on START while the map loads sends nothing) and a cancelled start unlocks it.
- **The Play online page** (`web/four.html`): its hints say that the first player in the room can start early with START once at least 2 players are in.
- **A test hook**: `--start-when N` (2 to 4, off by default) makes a client that leads a server's room press START itself once N players are in (a headless client has nobody to click); `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` uses it.
- **Review fix 1 (HIGH): one connection could peg the server, and nothing dropped it.** A StartRequest that is ignored and counted is never an offence, and that is what a flood of them was: 5-byte frames (`u32` length + the type byte, 20,000 per write) from one raw connection to a real `ants_server` took 91 - 95 percent of a core, grew the process by **1.1 GB in four seconds**, and the sender was never dropped (before the leader, type byte 24 was a violation and the sender was out after eight). The same held for **every other valid message that nothing counts**: Ping, Pong, TurnAck, a Hash of a turn that was never sealed and Chat (70 - 96 percent of a core, **1.1 to 3.4 GB** in four seconds, chat lines relayed to everybody all the while), in the room and in a running match, on a LAN host as on the server. The cause was the transport, not the handling: the lobby takes at most 64 messages of a guest per update and a session 256, but **every `TcpConnection::poll()` read up to 256 KB and parsed every frame in it into an unbounded queue** (the WebSocket connection has had an inbox bound since v0.0.81; the TCP connection had none). Now: (1) **the TCP inbox is bounded** like the WebSocket's (4096 messages and 1 MiB; at half of either the socket is not read until the game has taken what is there, so the bytes stay in the kernel and TCP holds the sender back; nothing is lost or reordered, a 64 KB message in the middle of a million small ones arrives whole); (2) **a budget per connection** (`include/ants_net/flood.hpp`: a token bucket of 1000 messages a second with a burst of 1000, in `HostLobby::Config` and `HostSession::Config`; a real client sends a few dozen a second, the sequencer takes at most 640 commands a second): a message that finds it empty is not handled and is a **violation**, so eight throw the sender out (a flood of 3000 pings, pongs, acknowledgements, hashes or chat lines is answered or relayed a second's worth at the most, then the sender is out; 1500 a second is out after about two seconds; 900 a second for twenty seconds is not touched); (3) **the ignored StartRequests have an allowance of their own**, `kIgnoredStartRequestsAllowed` = 16 per connection (a double or triple click costs nothing), and **each one after the sixteenth is a violation**, so the 24th throws the sender out, in the room and in the running match (`HostSession::ignored_start_requests()`, which the room's status adds to the lobby's count). A leader who floods takes its start with it like any leaver and the next one leads. **Measured again** with the same raw client against the same kind of real server (20,000 frames per write for four seconds, the same eight cases): the sender is dropped after 0.01 - 0.05 s (StartRequest, Pong, TurnAck, Hash; the StartRequest flood leaves `ignored_start_requests` at 24) or after about 0.4 s (Ping and Chat: it got back 1012 pongs and 1014 relayed chat lines out of millions sent, and in the room also the `Reject`), the process stays at 2 - 4 MB and 0 - 1 percent of a core, the control interface answers in 2 - 3 ms (8 ms at the worst; before: up to 175 ms median and 240 ms at the worst in a running match), and a match in another room keeps its 20 ticks a second (17.1 in the worst case before). **Over a WebSocket**, the transport that browsers use, the inbox was bounded already (3 MB, 1 - 8 percent of a core) but the sender was never dropped either (92,928 ignored StartRequests counted in four seconds): it is dropped after 0.01 s and 0.4 s now. The experiments were made with scratch scripts (a raw client in Python); `test_server` S3.32 and the flood section of `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` run the same thing as tests, and run against the server of before the fix the script fails five of its 81 checks (six when the growth of the process under a ping flood passes 200 MB within its window, which it does on some runs).
- **Review fix 2 (LOW / MEDIUM): the quick help could press the leader's START.** START! of the quick help (529, 437) - (627, 464) lies under the setup screen's START (526, 439) - (624, 466). The reviewer started a match by accident with a double click within the double-click time (its second click pressed the new START) and with a held Enter (its auto-repeat, `SDL_KeyboardEvent::repeat` 1, reached the new screen). Now: **the leader's START ignores the repeat of a key** (Enter, the keypad's Enter, S and s; Up, Down, Q and X still act on a repeat), **on a machine that joined a room the presses that continue the click that closed the quick help are not given to the setup screen** (a press that SDL counts as part of the sequence, `clicks` above 1, or that comes within SDL's double-click time, 500 ms, of the closing click: SDL gives up its count when the pointer moved more than a pixel between the clicks, which a hand does; a press after that time that SDL counts as a first click begins a new click: it is the screen's and ends the rule, and so does creating the setup screen again; the release of a press that began on the quick help finds nothing pressed on the fresh screen and does nothing), and **the quick help's button is let go of when its window closes** (a key can close the window while the button is held). **The original's own screens are not changed, because the original does not protect them** (checked in `Ants.exe` with Capstone and in the decompilation, `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md` 5.61): its window class has no `CS_DBLCLKS` (style 3 at 0x10319b3), so a second click is an ordinary press (message 0x201, forwarded at 0x1031e17 - 0x1031e48), its key translation (0x1031bbd) never reads the repeat bit of a key message, its input table (0x103078d) turns every key press message, a repeat included, into an event at most every 50 ms, and the setup screen's key handler (0x1014076) takes the key id and nothing else. A held Enter or a double click that closes the quick help starts a local game, or a LAN host's match, in the original too, and does here; the tests pin both so that nobody guards them by accident.
- **The web files are revalidated, not downloaded again** (`docker/nginx.conf`): `.wasm`, `.data`, `.html`, `.css` and `.js` were served with `Cache-Control: no-store, no-cache, must-revalidate, proxy-revalidate, max-age=0`, `etag off` and `expires -1`: a response that may not be stored cannot be revalidated, so every reload downloaded about 9 MB. (The headers showed two `Cache-Control` lines, because `expires -1` makes nginx add its own `Cache-Control: no-cache` and an `Expires` date, and no `ETag`.) They now carry exactly `Cache-Control: no-cache, must-revalidate` (AGENTS.md rule 6; Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy and Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy stay), with ETags on and no `expires`: the browser keeps the file and asks with `If-None-Match`, an unchanged build answers **304 and nothing is downloaded**, a new build has another ETag and is fetched at once (under gzip the ETag is weak, `W/"..."`, and validates the same). Checked on the built web image (`curl -I` twice on `index.wasm`, `index.data`, `index.html` and `index.js`, the second time with the `ETag` of the first answer as `If-None-Match`): the first answer is `200` with an `ETag` and exactly one `Cache-Control: no-cache, must-revalidate` (no `Expires`; the two cross-origin headers are there), the second is `304 Not Modified` with the same `ETag` and the same headers; `/`, `/four.html` and `/changelog.html` carry the same header; a plain GET of `index.data` downloads 14,650,810 bytes and the conditional GET answers 304 with 0 bytes (a whole reload of the four files is 8.8 MB gzipped); under `Accept-Encoding: gzip` the ETag is weak and revalidates the same way (304, 0 bytes). Before, on the image of v0.0.92: `200` with two `Cache-Control` lines, an `Expires` date and no `ETag`, so there was nothing to revalidate with. `tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh` keeps the header from slipping back (a check of the three blocks of `docker/nginx.conf`).
- Tests: suite 2.11 (`test_lockstep`) grows from 40 to 45 tests, 117,541 assertions (N2.3b: the Room message with every value of the leader and every byte in the leader's place, the cross-check with the slot state, StartRequest as one byte and nothing else, 20,000 mutated Room messages; S2.5: a late StartRequest in a running match (up to the 16 that are free); N2.1 / N2.2 / N2.3 take in the new messages; **N2.37: the message budget, exact to the millisecond and across the wrap of the 32-bit clock (an hour of honest rates never refused, an hour at 1100 a second refused exactly the surplus); S2.6: floods of acknowledgements, hashes of turns that were never sealed, pings, chat lines and late StartRequests in a running server match (out after a second's worth, the others untouched and bit-identical), 256 messages of a client per update, 800 a second for twenty seconds untouched and 1500 a second out after two; N2.38: the same on a LAN host's match**); suite 2.12 (`test_lobby`) from 13 to 17 tests, 64,222 assertions (N4.10: the leader is the first guest welcomed and not the lowest seat, the very first Room message already names it, the earliest of those left leads when it quits, is kicked, is cut off or thrown out, the first Room message that shows the departure already names the next, a later guest never leads, none for a host with a seat, a room without early start or a bot; N4.11: only the leader is heard and only when the room can start, everything else is counted and costs nothing (up to 16 of them), a payload is garbage; N4.12: the leader's side sends one byte, only from an open room that it leads; **N4.13: flood control in the room: the 24th ignored StartRequest throws the sender out (from a guest, from the leader after the Start, from a LAN host's guest), the allowance belongs to a connection, floods of pings and pongs end after a second's worth, 64 messages of a guest per update, honest traffic and 900 a second untouched, 1500 a second out after about two seconds**); suite 2.13 (`test_tcp`) from 6 to 7 tests, 72,168 assertions (**N3.7**: a raw socket floods a connection with a million small messages and with 100 big ones while the receiver takes 64 at a time: the inbox never holds more than 4096 messages or about 1 MiB, the unparsed bytes stay under a message and a read, nothing is lost or reordered, a 64 KB message in the middle arrives whole, a hostile length behind the flood still fails the connection, a side that closes lets go of its backlog, what a peer sent before it closed is still delivered); suite 2.19 (`test_server`) from 25 to 32 tests, 10,219 assertions (S3.26: a room for four started by its leader with two players and played to its end, the referee and both clients agreeing, the status keeping 4 and showing 2, a player who is not the leader ignored, the late clicks of the running match counted; S3.27: one player alone, a room without early start, a full room that starts by itself; S3.28: the leader leaves and the next one starts the match, a leader who asked and left in the same breath asked for nothing; S3.29: a client that cannot load, the pause after a cancelled start, a map that some seats cannot play; S3.30: `early_start` and the leader through the control interface and demo rooms; S3.31: the same over real sockets; **S3.32: a raw client floods the real server loop over real sockets with StartRequests and with pings next to a running match: dropped within two seconds, no slow pass of the loop, the process idle again and not grown by 200 MB, the other room's clock kept**); suite 3.2 (`test_hud_layout`) from 906 to 946 checks (the leader's screen: the host's art and buttons, none of the guest's, the room's map and its fog, the guest's rows, Up / Down / Fog change nothing and tell nobody, START in every spelling asks, a locked screen asks nobody, Leave works; **a held START key: the leader ignores the repeat of Enter, the keypad's Enter, S and s, the original's screens (the local game, a LAN host) do not, Up, Down, Q and X act on a repeat**); suite 3 (`test_app_integration`) from 218 to 219 tests, 8,157 assertions (8,146 on Debian 12; **9.14c**: the original's local setup screen is left alone: the second click of a double click and a held Enter press its START as in the original, and the release of a press that began on the quick help starts nothing); suite 3.6 (`test_network_app`) from 26 to 33 tests, 602 assertions (N5.20: the real application as the leader of a room of the real room manager over TCP, the can't-go cue and nothing sent with nobody else, the screen kept and locked while the map loads, the match with two players; N5.21: a player who is not the leader keeps the guest's screen and sends nothing, the screen changes when the lead moves, the new leader starts the match; N5.22: `--start-when`; **N5.23: a double click on START! (a second and a third click that SDL counts, a quick click that it does not count) closes the quick help and starts nothing, a click after the double-click time starts the match; N5.24: a held Enter or S starts nothing, a press on the quick help that is released after a key closed it starts nothing, a fresh click a moment after the key starts the match; N5.25: what a click leaves behind is forgotten when the setup screen is created again; N5.26: a LAN host's screen is the original's, the second click starts its match**); suite 3.9 (`tests/scripts/test_ants_server.sh`) from 55 to 81 checks (a real server, a room for four, two headless clients, the first one starts it with two players; the hints of the page; **the flood section: a raw client floods the real server with StartRequests and with pings next to a running match: out about two seconds into the flood while it is still sending, the memory, the control interface, the other room's clock, idle afterwards; the revalidation headers of `docker/nginx.conf`**; run against the server of before the fix it fails five of its 81 checks, six on some runs). **Every new behaviour was checked by mutating the code in a scratch copy: 51 mutations for the leader (the decoder's checks, the order of the election and of the broadcast, who is heard, the counters, the sessions, the room's rules, the control interface, the screen, the hook), each one made at least one new test fail; and 42 for the review fixes (the counters, the budget and its numbers, the per-update bounds, every gate of the TCP inbox and the close, the status count, the repeat rule on each key and on every kind of screen, the repeat flag, the swallow rule, its count, its time and its scope, the memory of the click): 39 make at least one test fail, the 3 that do not remove one of three overlapping gates of the TCP read and parse bounds (each bound is also kept by another gate; removed together they are caught). The first round found two gaps, both closed by tests: the byte bound of the inbox, and a key that closes the quick help leaving no memory of a click**.
- Rewritten tests: **N2.2** of `test_lockstep` (it listed type byte 24 among the bytes that no message has; 24 is `StartRequest` since protocol 7, so it lists 25 and asserts that 24 is `StartRequest` and `MsgType::Last`), **N4.1** of `test_lobby` (it set the *last* byte of an encoded Room message to 4 to test the receiver's own seat; since protocol 7 the last byte is the leader's, so it sets the byte before it, and a second assertion sets the last byte for the leader's own range); no other test encoded protocol 6. **Rewritten by the review fixes**: **N4.11** of `test_lobby` (it sent 50 ignored StartRequests from a guest that does not lead, 20 late ones from the leader and 100 to a LAN host, and asserted that none was an offence; it now sends the 16 that are free, a triple click and 16, and asserts the counts that follow) and **S2.5** of `test_lockstep` (fifty late clicks to a running match became the 16 that are free, with the count asserted); the flood itself is N4.13 and S2.6. Extended, nothing weakened: `PointerRig` of `test_app_integration` (the count of clicks and key events with a repeat flag; its callers are unchanged), the two version assertions of 12.108, N2.1 (the Room message with a leader and the StartRequest join the round trip and the check that any byte missing or too many is refused), N2.3 (the fuzz seeds include both and the plausible type byte runs to 24), N4.1 (its fuzz includes the new messages and checks the leader of every Room that gets through), N4.2c (a Hello of protocol 6 is answered "version mismatch" by a LAN host and by a server), the room harness of `test_lobby` (a guest can ask for a seat), the bare machine of `test_network_app` (it can hold back its "loaded" report), and `test_network_app` now links the real room manager (`ants_server_core`).
- Documents: `docs/NETWORK_PORT.md` (protocol 7, the leader, the request rules, the status keys, the screen, the hook, the tests), README (the Play online text, the setup screen in a network room, the options table, the server's control calls and the leader paragraph, the feature tables, the suite rows), `web/four.html`, `docs/NETWORK_PORT.md` (Flood control, the quick-help rule, the TCP inbox), `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md` 5.61 (what the original does with a held key and with the second click of a double click: the window class, the key and mouse translation, the input table, the setup screen's key handler, with addresses), `docker/nginx.conf`, `STATUS.md`. The screens are the original's, their use for a server's room is the remake's.

## v0.0.92 - 2026-10-01 - The black bars of a wide window scroll the map, fullscreen keeps the mouse, a safety net for the widescreen work, the review fixes of the bots, and four games in the corners of their hills

Five things ship together; the classic 640 x 480 picture stays exactly what it was. **For the player**: a wide window (fullscreen on a wide monitor) no longer ignores the pointer over its black bars, and fullscreen keeps the mouse in the window. **For the widescreen work that follows** (a parametric screen layout, a 16:9 picture, a wider HUD, the wheel zoom, touch gestures): milestone M0, a new test program that pins what the classic 640 x 480 picture draws and what the pointer does, so that every later step can prove that the classic picture stays pixel for pixel what it is. **For the build**: GCC 12 now builds the whole project with warnings as errors. **For the computer players**: the 35 fixes of the adversarial review of bot milestone B2 (the view, the map analysis, the arena, `bot_arena`), and the worker bot of B3 on the fixed interfaces; nothing in the simulation changed. **For the page with four games and the four-window start script**: the games lie by colour, in the corners of their hills (black top left, green top right, red bottom left, blue bottom right). The pointer fix and the safety net were made side by side on v0.0.88 and meet here on top of v0.0.91; they agree: of the 307 numbers of the safety net **none changed** (the fix works on SDL events, which none of the fingerprints feeds through the application's loop; checked on macOS with clang and on Debian 12 with GCC 12; the reasoning is in [`docs/audit/M0_notes.md`](docs/audit/M0_notes.md)).

**The black bars and the mouse.** The game draws its 640 x 480 picture in the middle of a bigger window: fullscreen on a wide monitor, or a window of any shape, shows black bars beside or above the picture. The pointer over a bar used to count for nothing (no edge scrolling there, no cursor), and in fullscreen on a machine with a second monitor the pointer slipped off the screen at the edge, so the edge scrolling stopped.

- **A pointer over a black bar is the pointer on the nearest edge pixel of the picture** (`include/ants_app/pointer_clamp.hpp`, at the top of the event loop, so every screen gets it: the match, the setup screen, the quick help): pushing the mouse into a bar scrolls the map like the edge does, and the game's cursor stays drawn on the edge. That is what the original shows on the one monitor of its time: it switches the screen to an exclusive 640 x 480 mode, so the whole desktop is the picture.
- **Fullscreen keeps the mouse in the window** while the game has the focus: `--fullscreen` and a macOS fullscreen Space (the green button, Cmd+Ctrl+F) both do; Cmd+Tab, another window taking the focus or leaving fullscreen frees it at once. A window (also a maximized one) never holds the mouse, so several games on one screen and other programs stay reachable. The original never confined the pointer itself (it only had the one screen); with a second monitor its pointer could leave too.
- **When the pointer has gone, the map stops**: a mouse button released outside the window (after a drag that left it: macOS reports no "pointer left" then), or a finger lifted from a touch screen, no longer leaves the pointer on an edge where the map kept scrolling on its own. While a button is held (a rubber band, the minimap) the pointer stays on the edge as before. This also ends an older quirk: a tap in the picture's 5 px edge strip used to scroll until the next touch.
- **Limits**: the fullscreen toggle of a Linux window manager (F11, a menu) is not detected, so that window does not hold the mouse (start with `--fullscreen` instead); on X11 SDL waits up to 5 s for the mouse when another program holds it at that moment (a menu, a screenshot tool), and then gives up holding it for the rest of the run; the web build cannot keep the mouse in the page (the browser decides).
- **The documents**: `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md` 5.43 said the original clamps the pointer to 0..639 x 0..479; it does not. Verified with Capstone: `GetCursorPos` is stored as it comes (0x1030a80), the file imports no `ClipCursor` or `SetCapture`, the exclusive DirectDraw mode (`SetCooperativeLevel` 0x53, `SetDisplayMode(640, 480, 8)`) is what keeps the pointer on the picture, and the only clamp (the relative move 0x103071a) is reached only through an input flag that no code sets.
- **Tests**: `test_pointer_model` group pillarbox (the clamp, after which events the pointer has gone, the grab table: 428 checks, 387 before) and `test_app_integration` 7.8 (bars on every side, the exact position of a pointer on the picture), 7.8b (the setup screen and the quick help), 7.8c (a release outside the window, the web's release after a "pointer left", a release over a bar), 7.8d (touch), 7.8e (the grab, with a real window of SDL's dummy video driver): 218 tests and 8,138 assertions, 213 and 8,027 before (8,127 where SDL's dummy driver gives no window the focus, as with Debian 12's SDL 2.26: the focus part of 7.8e is not run there). 21 deliberate mutants of the fix are each caught. The suite's temporary files carry the process id (7.6b, 7.6c, 8.8, 9.12, 9.13, 12.9b: the names only, nothing else of those tests), so two runs at the same time no longer share them.

**M0, the view fingerprint.** The safety net of the widescreen / zoom / touch work: a new test program that pins what the game DRAWS and what the pointer DOES at the classic 640 x 480 picture. M0 itself adds no production code and changes no existing test.

- **Draw calls** (`hud.*`, `screen.*`, 135 fingerprints): a recording `IRenderer` takes every call of `HUD::render` (97 scenes: every local colour, the "Get ready" dialog, a selection of every ant type, the hill, the chat with entries and typing, the minimap with all four teams at every corner of the map and on maps of other sizes, fog of war, allied / dropped / absent score boxes, an ant-heavy view, the quit / options / quick-help / alliance dialogs, hovered and pressed buttons, the rubber band) and of the setup, network room, guest and results screens, with ALL its parameters in final screen coordinates (sprite ids, named sprites, rectangles, texts with position, size and colour, the HUD team, clips, the minimap's image bytes), as FNV-1a 64 over a canonical little-endian serialisation plus the number of calls.
- **The pointer** (`ptr.*`, `view.camera.*`, 98 fingerprints): for EVERY pixel of the 640 x 480 screen the step of `edge_scroll_step` (the 60 x 60 map at the default rate from a camera away from the borders and at each of the eight borders and corners; other rates and map sizes; a ring outside the screen), the cursor that `HUD::evaluate_cursor` chooses in 34 states and cameras (every cursor of the original is met), the minimap's zone, world point and scroll, the start view, the camera's limits and conversions, and what a press and a release do (which control reacts, what is selected, which command the HUD sends, what is heard) in 17 HUD, dialog and selection states, on the setup and results screens and in the application's own pointer gate, with every zone's edge found to the exact pixel.
- **Pixels** (`px.*`, 74 fingerprints): what the real software renderer paints on the 640 x 480 canvas: the playfield at the corners and edges of the maps (ants of every type, colour and facing, effects, score bubbles, fog, selection markers, hit point digits, all straddling the borders of the clip), the ten single-frame cursors at every edge of the screen, and whole frames of an `Application` (the loading screen, the setup screen, the quick help, the match screen). TrueType text is never part of a pixel hash (its pixels depend on the font library): the boxes where the classic picture has text and the frame-rate plate are masked with fixed rectangles. **The numbers are the same on macOS (clang, SDL 2.28) and on Linux (gcc 12, SDL 2.26)**, arm64 and x86-64.
- **The golden numbers** were computed at v0.0.88 and are stored in the test (not one of them changed at this release); a difference is reported as `FAIL [scenario]: expected hash ... / N, actual hash ... / N`. `test_view_fingerprint --print` writes the table for regeneration: deliberately, when a milestone changes the picture on purpose, never to silence a failure (the header says so); `--dump NAME` lists the calls of a scenario, `--save DIR` writes the canvases of the `px.*` scenarios as BMP files, `--list`, `--only PREFIX`. Nothing reads the wall clock, a random device, a file other than `ants.chd` and the original maps, or the network; about two seconds of CPU time.
- **It can fail**: a self-check feeds the recorder 47 parameters one by one (the hash changes when one of them changes by one), a swap and a dropped call, and checks that a masked pixel changes no pixel hash and any other does. 34 deliberate mutations of the production code in a scratch copy (a sprite one pixel off, a text colour, a clip rectangle, an edge-scroll band, two calls swapped, a call dropped, the minimap's origin, the playfield's origin, click zones, the pointer gate, ...) are each noticed by at least one fingerprint, except the ones that are equivalent (list in [`docs/audit/M0_notes.md`](docs/audit/M0_notes.md)).
- **What it cannot see** (written down, with every place that still hard-codes 640, 480, 442, 440 or the screen margins and whether a fingerprint covers it, in [`docs/audit/M0_notes.md`](docs/audit/M0_notes.md)): the glyph pixels of text, the frame-rate counter and the version text, the network overlay, the four animated cursors, the pedestal glow, the tile grid overlay, the audio listener, how SDL scales the canvas into a window, `save_screenshot`, and the event path of the pointer fix above (`clamp_pointer_event` and the rule for a pointer that has gone sit at the top of `Application::handle_events`, which no fingerprint goes through: `test_pointer_model` and `test_app_integration` 7.8 - 7.8e pin them). The hard-coded sites list gained the two constants of `pointer_clamp.hpp` and the renumbered lines of `application.cpp`.
- Files: `tests/test_app/test_view_fingerprint.cpp`, `tests/test_app/CMakeLists.txt`, `run_tests.sh` (suite 3.10 "View Fingerprint"), the README's suite table, `docs/audit/M0_notes.md`.

**GCC 12.** A build of the whole project with GCC 12 and warnings as errors was impossible until now: it stopped in the application library (two files) and in eleven test programs, on diagnostics that clang does not give.

- **`LevelData::DimensionProp`** (the `.width` / `.height` of a level, usable as a property and as a call): GCC 12's `-Wconversion` rejected every implicit use on a non-const object ("choosing `operator uint32_t&()` over `operator uint32_t() const`"). The application now uses the call form (`.width()` / `.height()`: five lines of `application.cpp`, two of `map_select.cpp`), and the class's const conversion returns a `const uint32_t&` like the non-const one does, which ends the diagnostic for the other 75 places, all in seven test programs (`test_assets`, `test_challenger_m1_1`, `_m1_2`, `_m1_it2`, `_m1_it2_2`, `test_movement_golden`, `test_render_parity`; several use the property form on purpose, `test_assets` 6 checks `.width` against `.width()`, so not one of those lines had to change; the class keeps both forms and both conversions). Proved in a Debian 12 container (GCC 12.2, SDL 2.26.5): `cmake -DANTS_WERROR=ON -DANTS_BUILD_APP=ON`, every target of the project, no warning; `test_pointer_model` 428 checks, `test_view_fingerprint` 307 fingerprints (the same hashes as on macOS) and `test_app_integration` 218 tests, 8,127 assertions pass there.
- **Existing tests changed, meaning unchanged**: the two version assertions of 12.108, and for GCC 12 seven lines in six test programs: a local of 4.6 renamed (it shadowed `loaded` of the suite's own function), the key of a conditional in `test_hud_layout` (`'w'` against `SDLK_ESCAPE` is now `SDLK_w` against `SDLK_ESCAPE`: the same key), two casts to `uint32_t` in `test_pointer_model` (the stored-panel scene), `8u` / `44u` where `test_assets` and `test_powerup_actions` add an `int` to a `size_t`, and a loop variable of `test_challenger_m1_2` that copies the string it would have bound to a temporary.

**Bots B2: the review fixes.** An adversarial review of milestone B2 (v0.0.87: the complete view, `MapInfo`, the arena) found 37 points; two were wrong sentences of the notes (already corrected), the other 35 are fixed here, each with a test that fails without the fix (every fix was also undone, one line at a time, to see the tests notice: 75 mutants, 74 caught). The fixes sit on top of v0.0.91, so the worker bot of B3 (v0.0.88) now uses the fixed interfaces (its own bullet below). Nothing in the simulation changed: every golden state hash is as it was, and so is every existing test, except a few that had written down what a finding proved wrong (AI1.3: the points of a lunchbox; AI1.7: the mean of 300 timings; AI1.9 and AI1.10: a bound that was one sample; AI1.18: the single start tile of the cost field; AI4.2: a tamper that only worked by coincidence; AI4.3: a title that promised a queue check, and the dead `food_deposited` field; AI3.1: the dead `food_deposited` field; and the two places of the worker suite whose numbers come from the walking costs of the start: the pinned TREASURE rows of AI3.12, regenerated, and the seed of AI3.5's whole TREASURE match). The review's per-finding table is in [`docs/audit/B2_notes.md`](docs/audit/B2_notes.md) ("Review").

- **What a bot sees is fairer.** The state of another team's ant is now what the screen draws: an ant that has just been ordered but still stands in its idle clip (its path is not delivered yet, or it waits behind a blocker) reads as idle, not as "walking" (the engine's label told who had been ordered); own ants keep the engine's label. The score box of a team that has dropped out or is not in the match is covered on screen, so the view says 0 for it. A lunchbox is worth 30 to every bot (the real value is what the dead ant carried, which its picture does not tell). `takes_orders()` is true for a swimmer diving into or climbing out of the water (the engine accepts orders then). The owner and the burn-out time of fire walls and bridges on the grid a bot is lent are listed as an accepted deviation, with the reasons (`docs/BOTS.md`). `predict_ack` is documented as what it is, the prediction of a person's click, which differs from `apply_command` in two ways (a special order onto a walking ant, a goal in the middle of a lake); the "600 of 600" wording is gone.
- **`MapInfo` follows the engine more closely.** `approach_now` takes the engine's dynamic rules: the team's own and allied bombs block the way, the queue row of a team that dropped out is open ground (`BotView::walk_context()` gives the ally and the dropped teams), and it works for a lunchbox dropped after the start; `field_now()` makes one cost field for many piles. The walking costs of a hill start from every walkable tile of its queue row (a rock on the middle tile no longer cuts a hill off from the map), from the FIRST hill of a team (as the engine picks it). The last step onto a pile or a power-up is checked like every other step: not onto water (a step into water costs the engine's 8,000), not into a mound, not onto another live team's queue row. Tile row 90 of a map taller than 90 rows cannot be walked into (the original's path finder never generates it: verified with Capstone and the decompilation, now in `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md`). The points of a map are summed in 64 bits. The header says what `approach_now` costs (27 to 254 microseconds on the shipped maps) and that the trip model overestimates a diagonal-heavy route by up to about 10 percent; the trip model now fits the nearest pile of every hill to -0.5 to +12 percent of the measured solo cycle.
- **The arena counts what it says.** The engine's `food_deposited`, `food_stolen` and `food_lost` are never incremented (they are 0 in every match): the result and the report (format 2) now carry `banked` (points credited at the hill) and `raided` (points taken by raids), counted by the arena. The arena refuses a map that the seated teams cannot play (`LevelData::validate(roster)`, as the server and the application do) and a seat that does not exist (an undefined shift for a seat of 32 or more is gone), and counts the news and audio items its queues held.
- **The worker bot (B3) uses the fixed interfaces.** `HarvestTask` asks the map as it is now with the seat's rules of the moment (`BotView::walk_context()`: its own and its ally's bombs block, a dropped team's queue row is open) and with ONE walking field per look (`MapInfo::field_now`, made when the first pile needs it, then one cheap `approach_now` per pile) instead of a whole field for every pile it asks about; the field of its hill for the ants that the start took for shut in starts from every walkable tile of the queue row, as the analysis of the start now does (it was the middle tile alone). New test AI3.22 holds both: the queue row of a team that dropped out opens the way to a pile (the worker sends its ants within a re-ask after the drop), and with a rock on the middle tile of the queue row an ant that was shut in is sent once it is free (undoing either line, or asking without the seat's context, fails it). AI3.1 checks the arena's `banked` and `raided` instead of the dead `food_deposited`: the worker banked its whole score, the idle seats banked and lost nothing. **The pinned table moves on TREASURE only**: every approach of the start is up to one step cheaper now, so every trip of the trip model is 6 to 16 ticks shorter, which flips near ties of the ranking by points per tick of the trip and lowers the pile cap (`trip / 90 + 2`) of five pairs of seat and pile there. 25 numbers of the three TREASURE rows moved, 6 of them beyond the 8 percent band; the table was regenerated with `bot_arena --write-baselines` and the bands stay; TINY, SMALL, MEDIUM, GAUNTLET and ISLANDS give the same table to the last digit. Seat 2 alone over the whole match went from 3300 to 3030 (Medium) and from 3325 to 3045 (Hard): at tick 9206 of seed 1 it chose between pile 14 (25 points a unit, a trip of 521 ticks) and pile 17 (15 points, 313 ticks), 4,798 against 4,792 points per 100,000 ticks, and pile 14 brought more; with the shorter trips pile 17 ranks first (5,050 against 4,950). That near tie was the surplus of seat 2 that B3 could not explain. Seat 1 of four workers went from 2188 to 2410 and from 2150 to 2380, seat 2 of four from 2235 to 2050 and from 2288 to 2080; the mean of the four seats alone over the whole match is 2977 at Medium and at Hard (3028 and 3031 before) and 2779 at Easy (2784). The same shift swaps the first two piles of TREASURE seat 0 (equal points: trips 175 and 178 before, 169 and 162 now), and its carrier no longer jams in front of the gate around tick 240 (seeds 1, 2, 3, 9, 10 and 12 at Medium did before; now, of seeds 1 to 12, only seed 5 at Hard and seed 7 at Easy jam, late in the match). So AI3.5's whole TREASURE match is seat 0 at Hard, seed 5 now: a carrier stands on the tile in front of the gate from tick 6677, the clock sends it home at tick 7588, the seat scores 2940 (2845 when it is left standing); the window on the rescue tick is 30 ticks wide where it was 700, so a clock of 800 or 1000 ticks fails it.
- **`bot_arena` handles its report and its limits properly.** The report file is opened before the first match (an unwritable path costs nothing), written match by match, flushed, closed and checked afterwards: a report that could not be written completely is exit 2, not a truncated file with exit 0, and an empty `--out` is a usage error. A map given as a path that does not exist is named by its file name in the report (no folder of the author's machine). More matches than the limit are refused before a single one is built, a match keeps its numbers and not its command log, and a seed list that wraps at 4,294,967,295 is described correctly.
- **Tests**: suite 2.20 grows by 25 tests to 71 (319,177 assertions; `tests/test_ai/test_ai_b2fix.cpp`: the view against what is drawn, the swimmer and stunned states, dropped teams, lunchboxes, a differential of `predict_ack` against `apply_command`, a non-interference test (two engines that differ only in what other teams hide give identical views), the clock and the roster, the dynamic rules, the queue row, the first hill, the last step, row 90, lunchboxes, sums, the conformance cases the mutations found open, the walking time, the sink latency of the arena (even ticks, delay, canonical order, bots look after the turn is applied, a pinned hash), the result fields against the engine's getters with bots that harvest and fight, refusals, the queue counters, the replay's checks, the score ledger). The self-test of `bot_arena` (suite 2.21) runs the tool end to end on a scratch folder (exit codes, the report written and read back, a size limit that breaks the write) and checks its own wiring: 56 checks became 138 (the 52 of B2 and the 4 of B3, 81 of the review, and one because `--repeat` now compares B3's `reachable_units_left` too). Suite 2.22 (the worker) grows from 21 to 22 tests (AI3.22, above; 4,403 assertions: the count also follows the commands of the whole matches it plays, which changed on TREASURE). AI1.7 no longer fails on a busy machine (the fastest of 300 looks instead of their mean), and the bounds of AI1.9 and AI1.10 on the engine's path finder are a sanity bound, not a sample. `ANTS_TEST_FILTER=text` runs only the cases whose title contains the text; `run_tests.sh` clears it for suites 2.20 and 2.22 (as it clears `W_ONLY` and `W_SKIP`: a forgotten filter must not turn the master run green), and `test_ai` fails when a filter leaves no test.
- **Documents**: `docs/BOTS.md` (what a bot sees and what it may see more of, `predict_ack`, `MapInfo`, the report, the measured limits, the TREASURE rows of "Measurements" and why they moved), `docs/audit/B2_notes.md` ("Review", with "On top of B3"), `docs/audit/B3_notes.md` (where to find what moved), `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md` (row 90), README, STATUS.md.

**Four games in the corners of their hills.** The owner asked: "when you display 4 games on one page please put green top right, red bottom left and blue bottom right, black top left". That is how the four hills lie on the Small and Treasure maps (read from the map files: Green's hill top right, Red's bottom left, Blue's bottom right, Black's top left). Tiny and Islands put Green in the top left quarter and Red in the bottom right one (Blue and Black take the other two, in opposite ways on the two maps), Medium and Gauntlet lay their hills out differently, so this is one fixed layout for every map, not one that follows the map. It is used on the Play online page and in the native four-window rig. Nothing else changes: not the simulation, the network protocol or a state hash.

- **The Play online page (`web/four.html`) places the games by colour**, whatever order the seats were started in ("Play here" seat by seat, "All seats on this page", `play=here`): **Black (seat 3) top left, Green (seat 0) top right, Red (seat 1) bottom left, Blue (seat 2) bottom right.** With fewer seats on the page the colours keep that order without holes (a three-player room: Green and Red in the first row, Blue below Green; Green and Red alone: side by side; Black and Blue alone: Black left, Blue right), and the single column of a narrow screen (760 px and less, the existing media query) shows them top to bottom in it. It is CSS only: the cell of each game carries `data-seat` and takes its place in the grid's reading order from it (`order`). No frame is moved in the page, because a moved frame loads its game again and the seat would leave the match. The seat rows of the room panel stay in seat order (Green, Red, Blue, Black); the seats, the focus frame, the tick and hash labels and the in-step monitor are indexed by seat and are untouched.
- **The native rig (`start_game.sh` / `start_game.bat`, `--grid 2x2 --cell N`) uses the same arrangement.** `--cell` counts row by row (0 top left, 1 top right, 2 bottom left, 3 bottom right), so window 0 (Green, the host) goes to cell 1, window 1 (Red) to cell 2, window 2 (Blue) to cell 3 and window 3 (Black) to cell 0. Players, seats, colours, names, ports and who hosts are as before; only the cell changes (the host's window is now the one at the top right). Two windows (`--grid 2x1`) stay green left, red right; three windows stay green, red, blue in cells 0, 1, 2 (nobody is black, and the colours keep their order without holes, as on the page). The comments of both scripts and the README describe it (the README said that window 0 is at the top left).
- **Checked in the built-in browser**, on a copy of the page whose game frames were a stand-in game page (the layout and the page's own logic, not the games): every set of seats and start order (the 64 of a four-player room, the six start orders of a three-player room, the two of a two-player room: 72 cases) gave the colour order Black, Green, Red, Blue in reading order at 1280, 761, 760 and 375 px (the old page fails 55 of the 72); real clicks on the frames put the focus frame on the cell clicked; each cell's label and the in-step monitor follow their seats; no console errors.
- Tests: suite 3.8 grows from 93 to 98 checks (each window's `--cell` by seat for four windows, the four cells each used once, the cells of two and of three windows). **Rewritten: "window n sits in cell n"**, which encoded the old order of the cells (Green top left, Red top right, Blue bottom left, Black bottom right); the owner's layout replaces it, and each window's cell is still checked exactly. The new checks fail against the old script (5 failures) and the two and three window checks fail against a script that shifts the cells for every number of windows (2 failures). `start_game.bat` has no test (it cannot run here): it mirrors the shell script and `--dry-run` shows what it would start.
- Documents: README (the four windows: where each colour lies, the two-window row of the table, the suite 3.8 row) and the comments of `start_game.sh`, `start_game.bat` and `tests/scripts/test_start_game.sh`.

## v0.0.91 - 2026-10-01 - The original program leaves the repository

The owner's decision (2026-10-01): the licence stays open for all and the project cannot own the original game; of the original game the remake needs only the data archive, the maps and the music. So the original 1998 game's PROGRAM, its decompilation and the local tools of an installation of the original are no longer part of the repository. Nothing in the game, the server or the network protocol changed but the version number; every golden state hash is as it was and every existing test case still runs and passes (with the program present; without it see below).

- **What left** (no longer tracked, listed in `.gitignore` as "the original program and local tools: keep your own copy here, never commit it"): the program `Original-Ants/Ants.exe`, its Ghidra decompilation `docs/legacy/Ants.exe.c`, cnc-ddraw (`Original-Ants/ddraw.dll`, `ddraw.ini`, `cnc-ddraw config.exe`), the libretro shaders (`Original-Ants/Shaders/`, 15 files) and the original's chat transcript `Original-Ants/chat.txt`. A new clone does not contain them. **Pulling this change into an existing checkout deletes those files from its working tree** (Git removes the files a commit stops tracking): copy them aside first, or restore them afterwards with `git restore --source=<the commit before this change> --worktree -- Original-Ants/Ants.exe docs/legacy/Ants.exe.c Original-Ants/ddraw.dll Original-Ants/ddraw.ini "Original-Ants/cnc-ddraw config.exe" Original-Ants/Shaders Original-Ants/chat.txt` (they are ignored afterwards; this works only while the history still holds the files, see "Still in the history").
- **What stays**: of the original game exactly `Original-Ants/ants.chd`, `Original-Ants/Maps/*.LVL` and the music (`Original-Ants/*.mp3`, `*.MID`); the bundled Libre Franklin font (not from the original); the data derived from `ants.chd` (`asset_catalog/`, `docs/chd_table4_animations.json`); and the project's own code and documents, including the numbers re-derived from the program that the game is built from (the generated tables `src/ants_sim/movement_tables_data.inc` and `src/ants_app/minimap_tables.inc`, the texts of `src/ants_sim/game_strings.cpp`), the call-graph metadata `docs/binary_analysis.json` / `docs/ORIGINAL_BINARY_MAP.md`, the addresses, and the citations "Ants.exe.c line NNNN", which now point at a local decompilation. A survey of every tracked file found no other byte copy or dump of the program: a search for any 32-byte run of `Ants.exe` (runs with fewer than 8 different byte values left out) in every file tracked at this commit finds only the five strings that `docs/binary_analysis.json` lists as the texts its functions reference (call-graph metadata, kept) and two runs of 33 and 37 bytes of colour-palette entries that the kept data archive `Original-Ants/ants.chd` also holds, and no raw disassembly listing or byte dump is tracked (the audit notes say their scratch dumps were never kept).
- **The tests keep their full strength without the program: pinned digests.** `test_movement_tables` (suite 5) and `test_movement_differential` read static tables of `Ants.exe`. Every region they read is now pinned in `tests/common/original_program_bytes.hpp` by its virtual address, its size and the SHA-256 of its bytes exactly as the program holds them (45 regions, 2,675 bytes, all in `.text`: 38 regions of 1,331 bytes that the remake's tables reproduce and the 7 lists below, 1,344 bytes), together with the layout they read through (image base 0x01000000 and the three sections of `docs/GAME_REVERSE_ENGINEERING.md` 2.1). With a local copy of the program the bytes come from it: its layout and every region are checked against the pins (a mismatch is a different program: test 5.0 / 0.1 fails) and everything runs exactly as before. Without it the bytes come from the remake's own generated tables, serialised in the program's layout (the stored directions 0..4 of each colour-0 row, little endian, with the instruction bytes around the three immediates that the tests read); they must hash to the same digests, which proves that they are byte for byte the original's, and exactly those bytes are what the tests then read. A test can read only bytes of the pinned regions it named (anything else throws), so a new read needs a new pin; a pin is only changed with the program present. The SHA-256 implementation is checked against five FIPS 180-4 known answers, and each test prints which source it used ("Ants.exe" or "the remake's tables, verified against the pinned digests of Ants.exe").
- **What needs the program itself, and is skipped without it**: test 5.1 (the PE header: it is not part of the remake's data; the layout is pinned instead) and, in 5.3, the reading of the original's terrain-pair list and six tile-flag lists of `FUN_0100724c`. Those lists are the only regions the remake's data cannot reproduce: it keeps their per-tile result (`kTileTerrain`, `kTileFlags`), not their order nor the 25 explicit grass entries of the pair list. Without the program 5.3 checks the two per-tile arrays against pinned digests of the arrays that the original's lists produce, and those pins are verified by the run with the program.
- **Totals**: `test_movement_tables` with `Ants.exe` 31 test cases and 120,637 assertions (before: 30 and 120,582; the new test 5.0 and two digest assertions in 5.3), without it 30 test cases plus 1 skipped and 117,404 assertions; `test_movement_differential` with `Ants.exe` 4 tests and 1,034 assertions (before: 3 and 1,019; the new test 0.1), without it 4 tests and 1,029, with the same paths, walks and position changes as before. Proven by mutation: one byte changed in a generated table fails the run without the program at the digest check (5.0 and 0.1, which then does not run the models; a changed tile flag fails 5.3's digest, which no other check of the two programs notices without the program); a changed pin fails the run with the program.
